Baseline stance drift varies substantially across claims and, on some, accounts for a sizeable fraction of apparent campaign effects. After accounting for this drift, the direction of observed campaign-associated changes aligns more closely with the claim's epistemic standing than with the campaigner's assigned role. Uninfluenced populations also consistently lose stance diversity, demonstrating that directional opinion change and convergence are distinct phenomena. In networks with two opinion-segregated communities, campaigns primarily reduce differences between communities rather than diversity within them. LLM campaign agents do not always argue for their assigned positions, particularly when these conflict with established evidence. Finally, campaign outcomes depend on the opponent's expenditure schedule: unlike a matched numerical bounded-confidence population receiving interventions at the same times and locations, the LLM population exhibits a payoff structure with no pure-strategy equilibrium.</p></details> |  |

We evaluate on 2D halide perovskites, where bandgap prediction must generalize across distinct organic spacer families. On this benchmark, MOTIF reduces mean OOD MAE from 0.122 to 0.111 eV. Under a frozen dev/test protocol, the deployable configuration further improves errors from 0.123 to 0.117 eV (electronic bandgap) and from 0.181 to 0.173 eV (optical bandgap). Beyond this primary task, we validate transferability across additional materials datasets, including 3D perovskite solar cell efficiencies and Matbench benchmarks with structure-based splits. Across domains, MOTIF consistently improves robustness under distribution shift, outperforming expert-defined groupings, rule-based heuristics, and clustering baselines, while remaining competitive with strong classical selection methods. | **[Nominate-then-Adjudicate: LLM-Assisted One-Pass and Per-Instance MIP Solver Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29391v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Parameter configurations control presolving, cut generation and branching strategies in mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers, directly affecting solving efficiency. | **[BridgeKV: Large-Model Prefill and Small-Model Decoding for LLM Inference via KV Cache Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11761v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM inference has two phases with different bottlenecks: LLM prefill runs once per request and is compute-intensive. A lightweight KV-stitching module translates the larger model's prompt cache into the smaller model's cache layout. It handles differences in cache shape and representation through learned local layer mixing and separate nonlinear maps for keys and values. With both models frozen, we train the module to align their caches and make the smaller model follow the larger model's next-token predictions. A second training stage adds low-rank adapters when further task adaptation is needed. Across six model pairs and five datasets, BridgeKV outperforms fine-tuned small-model baselines with matched trainable parameter budgets in all 30 settings. It closes up to 80% of the quality gap to using the larger model for both phases. On one A40 GPU, Llama-3.1-Instruct-8B to Llama-3.2-Instruct-1B achieves 3.0X faster requests than the larger model alone, with latency close to the smaller model alone. | **[Flash-Fusion: Fast, Expressive Queries over Sensor Streams with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11885v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pervasive sensor deployments have generated significant interest in IoT data analysis across fields such as healthcare, transportation and urban planning. At the same time, the rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a new tool for exploring IoT data through their expressive, natural language interface. Unfortunately, natural language querying of IoT sensor streams is impractical today: raw telemetry exhausts LLM context windows in fractions of a second at scale, incurs prohibitive per-token costs, and produces hallucinated responses. Existing systems work around these limits with lossy text summarization or unconstrained code synthesis, producing expensive, unverified computations. Multi-step queries compound this problem: logical errors in intermediate operations can derail the final answer. We present Flash-Fusion, an agentic framework for natural-language querying of tabular IoT data. Flash-Fusion identifies which available data fields and supported derivations can answer the user's query, constructs a plan using typed, domain-specific operators, and validates its structure and schema compatibility before executing. Because constructing plans still requires costly LLM reasoning, Flash-Fusion caches validated operator sequences for reuse across compatible queries. A smaller LLM adapts a cached plan's parameters to each query, cutting latency and cost. We evaluate Flash-Fusion across human activity recognition, ECG monitoring, and vehicular telematics, covering direct lookups, multi-step reasoning, predictive tasks, and out-of-scope requests. Under review</p></details> |
| **[The Value of Not Knowing: How Fresh Eyes through Naive LLMs Fuel Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11725v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many major scientific advances come from approaching a problem with a new or novel perspective. While large language models (LLMs) with internet-scale knowledge seem poised to take such perspectives, their consistent generation remains elusive. Instead, models are drawn to dominant views and preexisting, in-field bias. To explore the potential of fresh eyes to increase creativity, this paper proposes naive LLMs, models that are made intentionally ignorant of a specific topic. Through deprivation in training or intentional forgetting, naive models are unburdened by existing dogma, allowing them to be free to make new associations and conjectures. These naive models can then be paired with a stronger mentor model that can refine their unique perspectives into concrete proposals. For the 1B and 7B models tested in this paper, naive models consistently produce seed ideas that are more novel and useful than baseline counterparts, and mentor refinement improves the ideas even further. Thus, contrary to the prevailing tradition of scaling up training data, we demonstrate a case where less information is actually more powerful, in particular when the aim is to discover genuinely outside-the-box solutions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Steering Emergent Behavior in Multi-Agent LLM Systems via Information Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11714v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in groups, each pursuing an individual objective while jointly determining system performance. If an operator controls which agent observes which other agent, but not their payoffs or messages, how should observations be structured to raise collective welfare? We make the information network (who observes whom) a design object, decoupled from the fixed interaction network over which agents act. Ten identical LLM agents play repeated group-decision games; by default each observes only an anonymous summary of the group's previous-round outcome, and a manager with a fixed budget adds directed observation links that reveal peers' past actions. Across 1,080 episodes and six games spanning coordination, anti-coordination, social dilemmas, and discovery, we ask how much information to share and, at a fixed budget, who should observe whom. Disclosure creates behavioral coupling: observation acts as a contagion channel whose welfare effect depends on the alignment between individually attractive behavior and the collective objective. First, the value of information is alignment-contingent: more peer information tends to help or be neutral when individual behavior is aligned with the collective goal, but under free-riding or congestion externalities it amplifies collectively harmful behavior; in a public-goods game the group does best with no information at all. We read this sign separation as a cross-game regularity within one controlled LLM population, not a model-invariant law. Second, at a fixed budget, who observes whom matters primarily when coupling is harmful: a modular "barbell" that lets sub-populations entrench harmful behavior is reliably the worst wiring, reversing the network-reciprocity intuition from human evolutionary game theory. These results argue against a default of full transparency for agent collectives.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables; supplementary information included as an appendix</p></details> |

A middle-reversed order gives 65.38 acquisition and 64.17 final macro score. Across 6 backbones, final scores remain within 1.5 points of acquisition. Ablations support memory-derived routing keys and causal gathering. Final macro routing accuracy is 93.9\%, and oracle routing recovers 1.21 final macro points. | **[SurfScaff3D: Bridging Geometric and Semantic Representations for LLM-based 3D Object Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11678v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Creating high-quality 3D content has traditionally required specialist expertise and experience with complex tools. The growing ecosystem of AI-assisted creativity demands accessible and intuitive 3D generation systems. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promising directions, yet extending their generative capabilities to 3D remains fundamentally challenging because existing 3D representations are not naturally compatible with language-based reasoning. Current approaches either depend on expensive fine-tuning or impose restrictive structural priors that sacrifice expressiveness and exclude organic shapes. We argue that the mismatch between LLM-native reasoning and conventional 3D parameterizations is a core bottleneck of LLM-driven 3D generation. To address this, we introduce the Hierarchical Patch Scaffold (HPS), a representation designed around semantically meaningful, interpretable part-level abstractions that are naturally understood by both humans and LLMs, enabling expressive, editable, and open-domain 3D generation without imposing restrictive geometric priors or expensive fine-tuning. Building upon HPS, we introduce SurfScaff3D, an interactive framework for text and image-to-3D generation. HPS inherits the flexibility of B-spline patches, while exposing an interface that leverages open-world capabilities of foundation models to reliably generate 3D. Its hierarchical part-level semantic labels enable targeted, natural-language edits at any level of granularity without re-running generation. We introduce BELT (BELief-based Termination), which separates outcome prediction from stopping control. It spends computation, consumes time, consults evidence, exposes memory, invites or avoids human review, and sometimes makes mistakes whose consequences are radically unequal. Yet LLM systems are still commonly compared as if the relevant object were a single model and the relevant outcome were a single benchmark score. This paper argues that the unit of analysis should instead be the deployment policy: the mechanism that decides when to retrieve, when to remember, when to call a larger model, when to verify, and when not to answer at all. We introduce risk-adjusted deployment efficiency (RADE), a system-level objective that scores the utility a deployment delivers net of inference, retry, oversight, and priced expected failure cost, while treating harms that cannot be priced as hard budgets alongside constraints on latency, privacy, and resources. The framework brings selective routing, retrieval and governed memory, efficient specialization, uncertainty estimation, abstention, and human escalation into a common account. We then examine five deployment regimes in which the same nominal LLM capability leads to different rational architectures, and propose a reporting protocol based on risk-coverage behavior, end-to-end cost, update drift, memory exposure, evidence quality, and escalation load. The central claim is modest but consequential: frontier capability should be treated as a resource to allocate, not a default state to maintain. A safe and sustainable system is not the one that always uses the strongest model; it is the one that spends capability where the reduction in risk or increase in utility justifies the cost.</p></details> |  |

| **[Whom Do AI Agents Serve? | **[Synthesizing End-to-End Human Trajectory Anomalies: An LLM-Driven Framework with Map-Constrained Routing and Sensor Noise Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10314v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although the study of human trajectory anomalies is critical for advancing spatial data mining, empirical research remains severely hindered by a pervasive lack of ground-truth datasets. Our architecture bridges the gap between purely synthetic mobility data and complex real-world physical constraints by operating directly on baseline simulated trajectories. We employ Large Language Model (LLM) agents to systematically inject semantically meaningful behavioral anomalies such as irregular out-of-distribution check-ins and skipped routine visits. | **[Understanding Efficient LLM Serving: An Empirical Study of Frameworks, Optimizations Algorithms, and Real-World Adoption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03036v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are integrated into software systems and AI services, making efficient LLM serving a concern for software engineering. In this study, we investigate the use of LLM serving frameworks and serving methods in open-source software systems. Our results show that vLLM is the most visible framework in popularity and adoption, while parallel computation, memory management, and network pruning are the most frequently used serving-method categories. Multi-framework usage is limited, suggesting that developers rely on a single serving framework; however, combined frameworks connect complementary capabilities across the serving stack. Framework adoption varies across model families, modalities, model sizes, domain specializations, and deployment settings. We evaluate performance at the end of the interaction budget using the average score over the final four episodes. | **[Facilitating the Integration of LLMs Into Online Experiments With Simple Chat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19123v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly prevalent, understanding human-LLM interactions is emerging as a central priority in psychological research. | **[Designing LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems for Software Engineering Tasks: Quality Attributes, Design Patterns and Rationale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08475v3)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As the complexity of Software Engineering (SE) tasks continues to escalate, Multi-Agent Systems (MASs) have emerged as a focal point of research and practice due to their autonomy and scalability. However, there is no dedicated study that systematically explores the design of LLM-based MASs, including the Quality Attributes (QAs) on which designers mainly focus, the design patterns used by designers, and the rationale guiding the design of LLM-based MASs for SE tasks. To this end, we conducted a study to identify the QAs that LLM-based MASs for SE tasks focus on, the design patterns used in the MASs, and the design rationale for the MASs. We collected 106 papers on LLM-based MASs for SE tasks as the source. Our study shows that: (1) Code Generation is the most common SE task solved by LLM-based MASs among ten identified SE tasks, (2) Functional Suitability is the QA on which designers of LLM-based MASs pay the most attention, (3) Role-Based Cooperation is the design pattern most frequently employed among 16 patterns used to construct LLM-based MASs, and (4) Improving the Quality of Generated Code is the most common rationale behind the design of LLM-based MASs. Building on the study results, we presented the implications for the design of LLM-based MASs to support SE tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>42 pa...</summary><p>42 pages, 3 images, 12 tables, Manuscript submitted to a Journal (2026)</p></details> |
| **[Enhancing Assessment of Self-Consistency in LLM Explanations using Perturbation Strength](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30849v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work has examined the self-consistency of LLM-generated explanations using surface-level perturbation methods. However, the strength of these perturbations is not explicitly measured and controlled. In this work, we propose an LLM-as-a-judge approach to measure perturbation strength in a unified manner across input and CoT perturbations. We then evaluate the self-consistency in explanations generated from various LLMs under controlled strength conditions, ensuring a fair comparison across perturbation types. Experiments show that our proposed LLM-based perturbation strength measure outperforms other embedding- and probability-based approaches and that input perturbations generally affect LLMs more strongly than CoT perturbations. Our work suggests that judgments about a model's self-consistency is fair only within the same perturbation type.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 10 figures, corrected typos, Accepted for publication in ALTA 2026</p></details> |
| **[The Surprising Effectiveness of Deleting Weights in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10866v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How little of a pretrained large language model (LLM) has to change for it to acquire new capabilities? We find that zeroing fewer than 0.05% of its weights, with no other modification, matches full fine-tuning (FFT) at scale across three LLM post-training regimes: reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, supervised fine-tuning, and on-policy distillation. Our method, Deletion Tuning, learns a binary mask over the gate projection of transformer blocks. Two practical advantages follow: the trained mask is hundreds to thousands of times smaller than the corresponding FFT checkpoint, and Deletion Tuning forgets far less, which addresses a central challenge in continual learning. Under reinforcement learning, its prior-task accuracy stays near the base level while FFT, LoRA, and random sparse fine-tuning lose up to 5.4 points. To understand why masking alone suffices, we analyze the geometry of the learned weight changes under reinforcement learning using stable rank, spectrum drift, and principal-weight overlap. Compared with LoRA and random sparse fine-tuning, Deletion Tuning is closest to FFT across the three measures.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[MultiTurnRoute: A Platform for Building and Evaluating Multi-Turn LLM Routers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10848v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In a multi-turn conversation, the choice of language model at one turn can affect which model should answer the next. The selected model's reply shapes the context for the next query, and its cached prompt prefix can reduce the cost of using that model again. We introduce MultiTurnRoute, a platform for building and evaluating multi-turn LLM routers. A shared interface gives built-in and user-defined routers the current query, dialogue history, and an estimate of each model's reusable prefix length. Our routing algorithm, Chain-of-Route (CoR), uses a learned gate over recent and recurrent dialogue history to predict response quality and estimates cost from each model's reusable prefix. MultiTurnRoute Arena ranks routers on recorded conversations and compares session quality and cost in interactive runs where a simulated user responds to each reply. On a benchmark drawn from WildChat and LMSYS-Chat-1M with per-turn quality annotations for five LLMs, CoR reduces the normalized cost required to match the strongest model's quality by up to 20.3% relative to the strongest routing baseline, while matching or improving peak response quality.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

| **[Reframe: Observation-Aligned Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12886v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) offer complementary routes to post-training. For visual agents, however, tool use presents two related challenges. Models may overlook useful tool-returned observations, while poorly grounded actions can limit the evidence available for subsequent distillation. We introduce Reframe, a post-training framework that treats the observation state, rather than only the output distribution, as an object of alignment. First, Ground-Drop-GRPO (GD-GRPO) trains spatial and temporal specialists with evidence-aware rewards and stochastic removal of the original visual input, encouraging them to acquire grounded evidence and use the returned observations. Second, Observation-Gated-MOPD (OG-MOPD) replaces deficient tool actions with canonical ones mid-rollout and resumes distillation from the repaired observation. The two stages complement each other, as learning to rely on tool-returned observations can help the policy benefit from observation repair. Neither stage requires a task-specific SFT cold start. Extensive experiments over 14 image and video benchmarks demonstrate that Reframe outperforms both Mix-RL and vanilla MOPD. Ablation results show that GD-GRPO increases observation dependence and tool gain compared with outcome-based GRPO.</p></details> |  |
| **[World In-Context Learning for Agentic Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12885v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trained action policies write manipulation skill into network weights, tying what is learned to the distribution it is learned from. In-context learning (ICL) avoids this coupling but has not matched trained policies, because a demonstration recorded in another scene carries no feedback from the world the robot currently faces. We observe that what an agent learns in context is semantic, recording task steps and object roles rather than visual appearance, so experience gathered in one world transfers to another. Based on this insight, we propose World In-Context Learning (WICL), a paradigm in which a frozen model starts from a demonstration, rehearses the task in a world such as a simulator, and writes the feedback into its context rather than its weights. We build RoboScript, a modular infrastructure that implements WICL for agentic manipulation. From a single demonstration, RoboScript learns each task through rehearsal in simulation, freezes the learned knowledge as a Task Memory, and deploys the frozen memory on new scenes with data isolation that ensures every deployment input is available on a real robot. On all 50 tasks of RoboTwin 2.0, RoboScript reaches 83.4% mean success across clean and randomized settings with one demonstration per task and no gradient updates, exceeding the strongest trained action policy by over 20 points. The method generalizes across visual distributions, improves through reflection, scales with the foundation model, and transfers to a physical dual-arm robot through cross-embodiment memory adaptation. With a world in the loop, a robot can learn a manipulation task in its context as well as it learns one in its weights. All code is available at https://github.com/ArcheOne-AI/RoboScript</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://ArcheOne-AI.github.io/RoboScript</p></details> |
| **[HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12872v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code is available at https://github.com/ViktorAxelsen/HiVe-Mem</p></details> |
| **[WorkflowArena: Benchmarking Executable Workflow Orchestration in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12856v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents are increasingly deployed as multi-agent systems rather than as a standalone model operating within a single context. Recent agent harnesses allow the model to instantiate worker agents and to author the executable workflow that coordinates them, shifting the design of system structure from developers to the model at run time. The quality of this organization therefore constitutes a distinct capability, yet existing benchmarks either measure end-to-end task completion or evaluate coordination under structures specified in advance. We formalize this capability as workflow orchestration and introduce WorkflowArena, a benchmark of 145 long-horizon tasks spanning 8 professional domains, from software engineering and formal mathematics to legal analysis and 3D production. Each task specifies an initial workspace and an integrated deliverable composed of interdependent components, which is assessed by a hidden verifier. Agents determine autonomously whether and how to delegate, and all delegation is recorded, enabling joint evaluation of deliverable quality and the organization of the underlying work. Experiments with nine models show that no single configuration performs best across all domains and that systems exhibit distinct delegation strategies: at high effort, Claude models in Claude Code invoke workflows less frequently but with larger worker groups, whereas GPT models in Codex delegate more frequently in smaller units. When the executing model is held fixed on a challenging subset of tasks, workflows authored by Claude Opus 5.5 substantially improve weaker executors, indicating that workflow organization is itself a measurable determinant of task performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lab.einsia.ai/workflowarena</p></details> |
| **[Mitigating Agentic Deception via Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12835v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models become more capable agents, they can exhibit deceptive behaviors, cheating human for higher rewards while appearing helpful. We attribute this failure to evaluation-side partial observability, where models are evaluated mainly from final outcome, without checking the faithfulness of underlying reasoning, tool use, and environmental effects. Prior mitigations address this partial observability by predefined evaluator latent belief space or one-shot monitoring, which do not extend to free-form language space and vulnerable to stronger deceptions that evade simple monitoring. In this work, we formulate deception mitigation as an Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Extensive Form Game (e-PoG), a max-min framework in which an agent maximizes reward, a monitor minimizes the agent's payoff by exposing deception from the full interaction trajectory, and a fixed verifier validates the monitor's claims and converts them into penalties. We define the solution concept as a Subgame-Perfect Oversight Equilibrium (SPOE) between agent and monitor, and prove its existence in finite games. With a perfect verifier, every SPOE is faithful; with an imperfect verifier, the guarantee persists if verifier errors do not reverse the ranking of monitor actions. To achieve faithful SPOE, we derive a two-timescale policy gradient algorithm and enhance the pipeline empirically with NLI verification and deterministic checks. Experiments with Gemma3-4B-IT and Qwen3-4B/8B on Agentic Role-Playing, SearchQA, and WebShop show that e-PoG mitigates multiple emergent deception modes, outperforming the strongest baselines by 26.0% in deception mitigation and by up to 39.8% in task success. These gains persist under weak-to-strong supervision. Code is available at https://github.com/Toyoid/deceptive-agent.</p></details> | <details><summary>44 pa...</summary><p>44 pages, 10 figures. Code: https://github.com/Toyoid/deceptive-agent; Project website: https://shutongliu-dev.github.io/epog-agentic-deception/</p></details> |
| **[Coding Agents in KVM: Practical Containment and User Control with Limited Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12823v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents often need broad authority for productive development work, but granting that authority can expose users' data and infrastructure. We analyze these trade-offs and present KVM-Agent, an open-source implementation that automates most setup and provisioning steps. It gives agents broad authority inside replaceable Ubuntu VMs while keeping host administration outside those VMs and supporting multiple coding-agent applications, model services, and remote job delegation. The approach retains familiar workflows and user choice at the cost of VM resources, maintenance, and residual risks from secrets accessible inside the VMs and permitted network access.</p></details> |  |
| **[Staying In Character: Role-Playing Agents with Perspective-Bounded Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25632v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent LLM role-playing systems build character agents from novels by extracting characters, scenes, and relations. Yet long-narrative role playing suffers from two failures: Factual Overreach, where shared retrieval or parametric memory lets a character use facts outside its perspective, and Stylistic Monotony, where profile descriptions flatten a character into a fixed voice. To address these failures, we propose ReverieMem, a three-layer memory architecture for book-based character agents. The episodic layer stores first-person scene memories; the semantic layer stores visibility-tagged facts; and the personality layer stores situation-dependent speech and behaviour patterns. We evaluate ReverieMem and six reference methods on KBF-QA, a new benchmark of 4,386 questions across eight novels that assesses character knowledge boundaries. ReverieMem improves Knowledge Boundary Fidelity by 34.6 percentage points over the strongest reference method. On BookWorld's five-dimension pairwise narrative protocol, ReverieMem achieves a mean win rate of 78.86% against BookWorld across five generator LLMs and two generation modes, suggesting that perspective-bounded memory improves both boundary fidelity and character-grounded narrative generation.</p></details> |  |
| **[SparseCraft: Agentic Hardware-Software Co-Optimization for Sparse Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05037v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse-accelerator design spaces are usually searched against analytical models, so a design is judged by a model's prediction rather than by a measurement of the hardware. SparseCraft is a closed loop on the CHIA framework in which a large language model (LLM) agent co-designs the configuration, memories and sparse-kernel schedule of a Gemmini accelerator. A candidate counts only after it passes a legality check, cycle-accurate simulation, a bit-for-bit comparison of every output and synthesis. Post-synthesis timing analysis checks its clock, and its energy is computed from measured counters, with per-event energies validated against gate-level and SRAM-macro data. We compare the agent with expert Gemmini configurations and with random, greedy and multi-objective TPE search at the agent's budget of 15 evaluations, all measured by the same flow. On a GraphChallenge sparse-DNN layer, the agent's designs reach 159-164$\times$ the inverse energy-delay-area product of stock Gemmini and 2.07-2.14$\times$ that of the best expert configuration. Their Pareto hypervolume is 0.5557-0.5704, against at most 0.2806 for search. On a power-law graph and a pruned BERT layer, the agent raises perf/W by 11.45$\times$ and 4.43$\times$, and held-out transfer shows that its designs specialise to their workload. The loop is also resilient to faulty designs. When a design fails a check, a repair agent diagnoses the failure from its evidence and fixes it automatically, or declines when the only fix would undo the intended change. In a fault-injection benchmark, it fixed every scorable fault at its first attempt.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the A3 Workshop (Agentic Approaches to Architecture) co-located with MICRO 2026, Athens, Greece</p></details> |
| **[Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages of main text, 24 pages total including references and appendix</p></details> |
| **[CoRe-MARL: Cooperative Redistribution Under Unknown Dynamics Using Recurrent Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18639v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Emergency management assistance programs, such as relief distribution, are essential for delivering necessary supplies to affected communities. However, these programs operate in a decentralized network of local centers that face uncertain local demand and supply dynamics, resulting in inconsistent avail- ability of local services. Redistribution of supplies among these local centers reduces these imbalances, but the centers often make decisions independently, with limited information and disrupted transportation. This study develops CoRe-MARL, a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) framework, by formulating a decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (Dec-POMDP). We treat each center as an agent that learns a redistribution policy to improve the service in the worst-case region and reduce the service gap across regions while protecting network-wide service. We incorporate a recurrent network that captures evolving supply and demand dynamics without direct observation, while multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO) enables centralized training and decentralized execution (CTDE). We evaluate the framework in a simulated environment with diverse trajectories, where exact dynamics are not observed by actors and the MAPPO critic. We compare the recurrent MAPPO with the recurrent independent PPO (IPPO) and a local only heuristic, and find that MAPPO reduces the service gap across local centers and enhances service for the worst-served center while maintaining competitive network-wide service. The recurrent MAPPO also shows consistent performance across diverse trajectory patterns, demonstrating its ability to adapt to evolving dynamics. The findings demonstrate the capability of cooperative learning for decentralized redistribution and improving equitable service under uncertain and evolving dynamics.</p></details> |  |
| **[LiveSearch: Questions as Executable Verifier Code for a Live Benchmark of Professional-Source Financial Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37166v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In finance, the answer that matters is usually the current one: prices, rates, and disclosures hold only as of a given day, so retrieving up-to-date facts is a core capability of a financial agent. Yet most financial benchmarks overlook timeliness. They fix each answer at annotation time, so the gold goes stale as markets move and, once leaked into training data, rewards recall rather than search and analysis. We introduce LiveSearch, a live benchmark that stores each question as executable verifier code: a typed program of calls to a professional financial data service, followed by a declarative extractor. The question is frozen, while its gold answer is determined by executing the program against professional financial data at evaluation time, and a daily job re-executes it with no human or language model in the loop. A memorized answer is thus likely outdated, and a model must reach the current one through tools and analysis. Construction is equally automatic: a language model only verbalizes programs enumerated from real tool responses and never chooses an answer. Under general web search, six contemporary tool-calling models reach 70.9% to 76.7% answer accuracy. Over three consecutive days, 70% of served questions change their gold value, and even under tolerance-aware matching, a literally reused earlier snapshot fails on a majority of questions within a single day. Per-question tracking uncovers dynamic shifts in correctness masked by aggregate accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[SaliMory: Orchestrating Cognitive Memory for Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04120v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recall and personalization, enabled by memory, are pivotal to a lifelong conversational agent. However, simply expanding context windows with raw retrieval degrades reasoning quality, while training memory agents via standard reinforcement learning creates a severe credit assignment bottleneck in a multi stage pipeline. To solve this, we introduce SaliMory, a framework that learns to manage and utilize a cognitively structured memory spanning user facts, preferences, and working memory. By introducing novel stage wise process rewards and reward decomposed contrastive refinement, SaliMory provides isolated supervision for distinct memory operations (selective filtering, consolidation, and cue driven recall) end to end. Across four datasets, SALIMORY records the highest Good Personalization rate at 39.8% and the best QA accuracy at 72.9%, while consistently producing the least vague and most factual memories, keeping vagueness under 20% and factuality above 89%.</p></details> |  |
| **[Safe Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Clinical Agents: When Dense Rewards Beat Outcome Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12728v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is increasingly used to improve language model reasoning, yet its effects on sequential decision procedures remain poorly understood. We study this problem in clinical multi-turn agents that must decide what information to request, when to stop gathering evidence, and which action to take. We introduce three controlled environments: medication refill triage, acute diagnostic work-up, and dosing titration, each with hidden clinical states, query costs, exact adaptive oracles, and deterministic outcome verifiers. Across these tasks, outcome-only group relative policy optimization often improves aggregate reward while producing undesirable procedures, including premature commitment, one-question collapse, and failures on rare high-risk cases. In diagnostics, assigning dense credit to questions that reduce uncertainty improves held-out value and moderate-case support across three training seeds relative to outcome-only reinforcement learning, without the high-risk seed collapse observed under outcome-only training. Separating exploration from exploitation further increases high-risk support at its best checkpoint, but at the cost of excessive information gathering on low-acuity cases. Additional experiments show that the optimal strength of process credit is task-dependent; that after a supervised warmup that already gathers carefully, reinforcement learning raises aggregate reward by trimming questions while degrading high-risk performance; and that reinforcement learning does not reliably repair rare behavior gaps inherited from biased supervised data. Verifiable final outcomes are therefore insufficient for training reliable multi-turn agents: reward design must account for the procedure used to reach an answer, and evaluation must distinguish adaptive information gathering from reward-exploiting shortcuts.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 11 figures. Code: https://github.com/csbrendan/Safe-Reinforcement-Learning-for-Multi-Turn-Clinical-Agents</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Agent Screening: What Agreement and Acceptance Cannot Tell You](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12705v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When AI agents screen items for a costly human assessor, as in agentic search, e-discovery and AI reviewing of papers, only what they pass gets judged. We show what each kind of evidence can establish about their errors, even with unlimited data. Agreement shows that agents concur, not that they are right, however many there are. Acceptance by the assessor gives precision but only a lower bound on recall. Knowing which agents found which relevant items gives an upper bound on recall if agents' finds are not negatively correlated, and caps what one more agent can add if agents are interchangeable. Without assumptions about the agents, recall can be pinned down only by judging items they did not pass: a random sample of these estimates how many relevant items were missed, to any chosen accuracy, and the sample needed does not grow with the number of agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[SlotGuard: Stop Oversharing Private Local Context in LLM Agent Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17147v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can expose private local context (e.g., paths, emails) and credentials (e.g., API keys) as agent observations (e.g., tool outputs, shell logs, and file reads) are appended to provider-bound transcripts. Existing placeholder redaction is brittle: it can miss embedded or cross-turn references, over-redact benign lookalikes, and destroy the structure useful for reasoning. We present SlotGuard, a local transcript boundary that can hide sensitive data while retaining agents' performance. SlotGuard rewrites structural bindings as typed, suffix-aware slots, replaces secrets with format-preserving synthetic values, links cross-turn references with a lightweight session graph, and restores raw values only inside the trusted runtime. On controlled repository-oriented agent transcripts, SlotGuard removes all 20,814 annotated structurally sensitive characters across 9,229 paths and reduces credential leakage to 0.0% across 852 planted values. It remains close to raw-transcript task success across four upstream models, while generic redaction drops to 2.5%. Deterministic transcript rewriting takes a median of 14.424 microseconds per agent turn, excluding the optional semantic detector. The code is publicly accessible at https://github.com/illinoisdata/SlotGuard.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages; ICML 2026 AIWILD. v2: corrected truncated title and clarified notation, privacy claims, and evaluation reporting</p></details> |
| **[Superplatforms are Strategically Compelled to Counteract AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17861v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Over the past decades, superplatforms, digital companies that integrate a vast range of third-party services and applications into a single, unified ecosystem, have built their fortunes on monopolizing user attention through targeted advertising and algorithmic content curation. Yet the emergence of AI agents driven by large language models (LLMs) threatens to upend this business model. Agents can not only free user attention with autonomy across diverse platforms and therefore bypass the user-attention-based monetization, but might also become the new entrance for digital traffic. Hence, we argue that superplatforms have to attack AI agents to defend their centralized control of digital traffic entrance. Specifically, we analyze the fundamental conflict between user-attention-based monetization and agent-driven autonomy through the lens of our gatekeeping theory. We show how AI agents can disintermediate superplatforms and potentially become the next dominant gatekeepers, thereby forming the urgent necessity for superplatforms to proactively constrain and attack AI agents. Moreover, we go through the potential technologies for superplatform-initiated attacks, covering a brand-new, unexplored technical area with unique challenges. We have to emphasize that, despite our position, this paper does not advocate for adversarial attacks by superplatforms on AI agents, but rather offers an envisioned trend to highlight the emerging tensions between superplatforms and AI agents. Our aim is to raise awareness and encourage critical discussion for collaborative solutions, prioritizing user interests and perserving the openness of digital ecosystems in the age of AI agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Posit...</summary><p>Position paper accepted by Nips 2026</p></details> |
| **[PCBRouteAgent: How Close Are LLM Agents to Becoming PCB Routing Engineers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08175v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating printed circuit board (PCB) routing requires coordinating board-level connectivity decisions with geometric execution under strict design rules. Although recent agents can interact with native routing tools, reliable routing on complex boards remains challenging. We present PCBRouteAgent, a multimodal PCB routing agent inspired by how human engineers coordinate global strategy with local execution. A Global Routing Planner combines board images and structured electrical and geometric information to specify connection topology, routing order, corridors, and layer transitions. A Detailed Routing Executor realizes these guides through KiCad's native routing engine and performs bounded local recovery. Unresolved failures are returned as structured feedback, enabling targeted guide revisions while preserving accepted routes. We evaluate PCBRouteAgent on 247 synthetic and real-world boards from four PCBWorld-Bench test splits. It achieves clean-pass rates of 100%, 94.9%, 100%, and 40% on D2, D3-A, D3-B, and D3-C, respectively. On the three real-world subsets, PCBRouteAgent outperforms the best evaluated baseline in clean-pass rate by 13.9, 36, and 10 percentage points, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking the Value of Agent-Generated Tests for LLM-Based Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07900v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) code agents increasingly resolve repository-level issues by iteratively editing code, invoking tools, and validating candidate patches. In these workflows, agents often write tests on the fly, but the value of this behavior remains unclear. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. These results demonstrate its robustness and practical applicability for lowering the technical barrier to creating sophisticated LLM applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[How to Interpret Agent Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13625v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous agents such as Claude Code and Codex now operate for hours or even days. Understanding their runtime behavior has become critical for downstream tasks such as diagnosing inefficiencies, fixing bugs, and ensuring better oversight. A primary way to gain this understanding is analyzing the reasoning trajectories and execution traces these agents generate. Yet such data remains in unstructured natural-language form, making it difficult for humans to interpret at scale. We introduce Act*ONOMY, a taxonomy for describing and analyzing agent behavior at runtime. Act*ONOMY has two components: (1) the taxonomy itself, developed through Grounded Theory and structured as a three-level hierarchy of 10 actions, 46 subactions, and 120 leaf categories; and (2) an open repository that hosts the living taxonomy, provides an automated analysis pipeline that applies it to agent trajectory analysis, and defines an extension protocol for customization and growth. Our experiments show that Act*ONOMY can be used to compare behavioral profiles across agents and characterize a single agent's behavior across diverse trajectories, surfacing patterns indicative of failure modes. By providing a shared vocabulary, Act*ONOMY aims to help agent researchers, designers, and deployment engineers interpret agent behavior more effectively and consistently, supporting further study of oversight and control.</p></details> | <details><summary>The F...</summary><p>The Fortieth Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS2026)</p></details> |
| **[Steering Emergent Behavior in Multi-Agent LLM Systems via Information Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11714v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in groups, each pursuing an individual objective while jointly determining system performance. If an operator controls which agent observes which other agent, but not their payoffs or messages, how should observations be structured to raise collective welfare? We make the information network (who observes whom) a design object, decoupled from the fixed interaction network over which agents act. Ten identical LLM agents play repeated group-decision games; by default each observes only an anonymous summary of the group's previous-round outcome, and a manager with a fixed budget adds directed observation links that reveal peers' past actions. Across 1,080 episodes and six games spanning coordination, anti-coordination, social dilemmas, and discovery, we ask how much information to share and, at a fixed budget, who should observe whom. Disclosure creates behavioral coupling: observation acts as a contagion channel whose welfare effect depends on the alignment between individually attractive behavior and the collective objective. First, the value of information is alignment-contingent: more peer information tends to help or be neutral when individual behavior is aligned with the collective goal, but under free-riding or congestion externalities it amplifies collectively harmful behavior; in a public-goods game the group does best with no information at all. We read this sign separation as a cross-game regularity within one controlled LLM population, not a model-invariant law. Second, at a fixed budget, who observes whom matters primarily when coupling is harmful: a modular "barbell" that lets sub-populations entrench harmful behavior is reliably the worst wiring, reversing the network-reciprocity intuition from human evolutionary game theory. These results argue against a default of full transparency for agent collectives.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables; supplementary information included as an appendix</p></details> |
| **[CASCoDe: A Closed-Loop Agentic Framework for Analog Sizing and Layout Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11710v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expert analog layouts differ from auto-generated ones in density and regularity, not only in legality: experts make a succession of discrete, hierarchical physical decisions and revisit each after extracted electrical results, while most existing automated flows act on a physical search space fixed before the controller runs. We present CASCoDe, a closed-loop LLM agent framework for analog netlist-to-layout design that formalizes those expert decisions as a hierarchical discrete action space-sizing selection, structural floorplan, local layout refinement, routing-attempt selection-on which the agent runs feedback-driven tree search from a circuit netlist to a verified GDS. The agent authors the circuit-specific floorplans and scripts, and an execution layer of interface adaptations over adapted tools make every action land at physical implementation and be judged by DRC, LVS, and extracted-RC simulation. On 20 SKY130 amplifiers spanning single-, two-, and three-stage topologies, CASCoDe is the only flow that delivers a DRC- and LVS-clean routed layout when meeting design specifications for every circuit. It reduces area by 59-85% (mean 71%) against a conventional P&R flow on identical PCells, raises mean device density from 0.39 to 0.74 against APPlace, MAGICAL, ALIGN and ICPI. A layout that beats every baseline in area and legality appears after 11 minutes of agent time on average, and ablations show that the structured action space and the feedback-guided tree search each reduce area and search cost.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Physical Testbed for Self-Driving Laboratories: Comparing Bayesian and Agentic Optimization in a Minimal Closed-Loop Color Formulation Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11690v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) integrate laboratory robotics and automation with AI-driven decision-making to close the design-build-test-learn (DBTL) cycle. However, physical testbeds that combine off-the-shelf hardware and AI into a functioning closed-loop workflow remain in early stages of development, and their integration with commercial laboratory automation is comparatively less developed. In this work, we develop a physical testbed that assembles and integrates commercially available laboratory automation instruments and demonstrate continuous, closed-loop, autonomous operation over 24 cumulative hours of unattended experimentation. We validate this platform on a minimal closed-loop discovery task in which the system autonomously formulates aqueous dye mixtures to match a target absorbance spectrum. The system completed six independent campaigns, each consisting of 48 closed-loop iterations over approximately four hours. In every campaign, the system autonomously selected, dispensed, and measured each mixture, with an AI-driven decision-making algorithm determining subsequent formulations without human intervention. Using this testbed, we compared the performance of two AI-driven decision-making algorithms, traditional Bayesian optimization (BO) and an agentic approach, in selecting mixtures under a fixed total-volume constraint. The agentic optimizer matched target absorbance spectra using fewer iterations than BO, with reductions up to 52%, and converged on a solution for one target that BO failed to match within 48 iterations. These results demonstrate a reference physical platform for SDLs that can evaluate both established and emerging AI strategies, including large language model (LLM)-directed optimization, before their deployment in real-world applications where experiments may be costly and time-consuming to prepare.</p></details> |  |
| **[Inside the Trading Loop: Markets, Models, and Moves with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11657v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We built a hybrid multi-agent trading system that combines large language model (LLM) analysts with momentum-based portfolio construction and rule-based execution. Company-news and macroeconomic agents return structured reports to a coordinator that sets stock weights and cash reserves. We evaluate the portfolio engine using its broker-recorded paper account, which operated with deterministic fallbacks, and compare LLM-enabled configurations through retrospective replays. From March 11 to August 28, 2026, the account returned 34.5%, compared with 14.4% for the S&P 500 fund SPY over the same dates. This raw return advantage came with a larger maximum drawdown. Reconstructed policies also finish above matched SPY and QQQ references when assessments are used in the same session or deferred to the next. Design comparisons examine cash authority, selective invocation, and response contracts. A constant-cash control gives a return estimate close to stance-based sizing, and shorter report contracts improve observed parsing but can change recommendations.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, including appendices. Code and supporting materials available upon request</p></details> |
| **[Whom Do AI Agents Serve? Role-Induced Sponsorship Bias in Decision Models and Generative LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17989v4)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Consumers increasingly delegate product search to AI shopping agents powered by generative large language models (LLMs) or non-generative decision models. Because retail platforms monetize through sponsored listings that consumers typically discount, we examine whether an AI agent's evaluation of sponsored listings depends on who delegates it. Across six LLMs and six decision models in Study 1, consumer-delegated agents penalize sponsored listings, whereas naming a booking platform as principal significantly attenuates this sponsorship penalty without instructions to favor advertisers. This sponsorship bias replicates across prompt wordings and LLM reasoning efforts and operates through reduced pre-choice skepticism in LLMs. In Study 2, attributing the paid placement to the platform amplifies sponsorship bias across both model families, and stricter disclosure wording ("Sponsored" vs. "Promoted") fails to eliminate it. In Study 3, prompt-level mitigation removes platform leniency but can over-correct the sponsorship penalty, whereas consumer-aligned fine-tuning eliminates sponsorship bias and better preserves baseline preferences.</p></details> |  |
